<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Ajna Chakra (the Third Eye center), specifically explaining Verse 35. The previous page discussed the practitioner's realization of non-duality and their supreme powers; this page focuses on the internal vision of the sacred syllable Om and the subtle sounds and lights perceived during deep meditation.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 45

## In this place, when the mind is absorbed in the house of the beautiful face...

The connection of the text is as follows: in this location, the **Pure-Minded Inner Soul** <term>shuddha-buddhy-antaratma</term>—which possesses a light and color resembling the thirteen vowels <note>The thirteen vowels in Sanskrit symbolize a complete range of subtle sonic vibrations.</note>—constantly resides. To further describe this, it is said to have "a light like a lamp" <note>original: pradipabha-jyotih</note>. 

One might ask: is this Inner Soul merely a color resembling the thirteen vowels? To this, the text replies: **"Above that is the crescent moon, and above that is the shining point in the form of the letter M."** <note>original: makara</note>. Therefore, by the union of the crescent moon <term>ardha-chandra</term> and the point <term>bindu</term> above that specific letter-form, the actual nature of the sacred syllable **Om** <term>Pranava</term> is established. 

The text continues: **"Above that is Nada."** This means that above the Om syllable, there is a subtle, intermediate sound. This sound is described as **"laughing at a mass of nectar-streams as white as Baladeva."** <note>Baladeva is Balarama, the brother of Krishna, traditionally depicted with skin as white as milk or snow. The "laughter" is a poetic way of saying the light of this sound is even whiter and more brilliant than Baladeva’s complexion or liquid nectar.</note> This implies that the sound <term>nada</term> is of an exceedingly white color. 

Some scholars read the phrase as "At the beginning of that is this Nada" <note>original: tadadye nado'sau</note> and explain it as "at the beginning of the point <term>bindu</term> which is the letter M." However, this interpretation is incorrect. If the point is already described as "shining above the letter M," then mentioning its beginning again would be redundant. Rather, this subtle sound is a specific part of the indivisible **Supreme Point** <term>parabindu</term>, distinct from the sound components that make up the audible Om, and it exists above the Om syllable. 

If one objects, saying that in a description of the Om syllable, one should only discuss its direct parts, and asks why an "extra" sound is being introduced, we would favor the reading "at the beginning of that is this Nada." But the meaning would then be: "This sound, shown at the beginning of the point-form letter M, is the one which laughs at the nectar-stream." Thus, the description "white as Baladeva" is applied to the previously mentioned sound, not as a new element, avoiding the error of redundancy. || 35 ||

---

Having defined the sacred syllable Om, the text now describes the method of its union <term>yoga</term> in the phrase **"In this place..."** The meaning is this: a practitioner of Yoga <term>yogi</term>, having secured the "city" <note>The "city" refers to the physical body and its various gates or senses.</note> and with great joy having completely blocked the "inner palace"... <note>This refers to the practice of closing the eyes, ears, nose, and mouth to turn the attention entirely inward.</note>

<summary>This page provides a technical commentary on Verse 35, describing the visual and auditory components of the Om syllable within the Ajna Chakra, specifically focusing on the brilliance and whiteness of the subtle sound (Nada).</summary>
<keywords>Ajna Chakra, Om, Pranava, Nada, Bindu, Baladeva, Inner Soul, Yoga practice</keywords>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 46

### Verse 36

**By practicing the well-known service to the Supreme Guru while maintaining the unsupported state, the Yogi sees sparks of the "friends of the wind" (fire). Then, within the center of that space, shining forms are also constantly seen.** <note>The "unsupported state" (Niralamba) refers to a high level of meditation where the mind no longer relies on external objects or thoughts.</note>

### Commentary

**By that method, one performs the Seal of the Womb.**
This means one performs the **Seal of the Womb** <term>Yoni Mudra</term>, which acts as the revealer of the inner chamber of knowledge. In this context, the word "chamber" <note>original: pur</note> specifically refers to the Yoni Mudra. Having performed this Seal here in the **Command Center** <term>Ajna Chakra</term>, and through the practice of repeatedly contemplating the sacred syllable **Om** <term>Pranava</term>, the mind becomes absorbed into the space surrounding that sound. 

Following this, within the center of the triangle that supports the Om, in the "inner sky" <note>original: antararikshe</note>, the practitioner sees "sparks of the friends of the wind." This poetic phrase refers to fire; the Yogi sees flashes of light resembling sparks of fire <note>original: jyotih-sphulingan</note>. These become directly perceptible to the mind’s eye. 

The text states that "binding the chamber" is necessary because, without it, the mind cannot achieve pure stability. Therefore, the characteristics of the **Seal of the Womb** <term>Yoni Mudra</term>—which is the cause of this binding—are described as follows:

"Then, one should place the left heel against the perineum <note>original: guhya; the area between the anus and genitals</note>. Above that, O Great Goddess, place the right heel. Keep the body, head, and neck straight. Form the mouth into the shape of a **crow's beak** <note>original: kaka-chancu-putena; a technique of pursing the lips to draw in air</note>."

"Draw in the external air to fill the stomach. Then, with the fingers, firmly close the sensory gateways <note>original: karanani</note> while remaining composed. Use the thumbs to close both ears, the index fingers for the eyes, the middle fingers for the nostrils, and the remaining fingers to close the mouth tightly."

"Having bound these, and reflecting on the unity of the breath <term>prana</term> and the mind <term>manas</term> while remembering the sacred mantra, one should properly retain the breath. This Yoga is beloved by all practitioners."

The sacred Scripture <term>Shruti</term> confirms that the mind's stability is gained through such a Seal. It explains that the mind naturally wanders among worldly objects like a restless swan; by restraining the breath through this practice, the mind itself is brought under restraint. 

Alternatively, "binding the chamber" may refer to the **Sky-Walking Seal** <term>Khechari Mudra</term>. This also produces the desired stability. As the tradition says: "Since the mind moves in the 'sky' <note>original: khe</note> of consciousness, and the tongue moves in the 'sky' of the palate, this is called the Sky-Walking Seal." <note>This Mudra involves folding the tongue back to touch the soft palate, which is said to lock the vital energy within the head.</note>

<summary>This page describes Verse 36, focusing on the Yoni Mudra and Khechari Mudra as essential techniques for stabilizing the mind in the Ajna Chakra to perceive internal spiritual lights.</summary>
<keywords>Yoni Mudra, Ajna Chakra, Pranava (Om), internal fire, breath control (Pranayama), Khechari Mudra, mental stability</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the visual and mental states achieved during meditation on the Ajna Chakra (the Third Eye). The previous page ended while explaining the Seal of the Womb (Yoni Mudra) and the initial appearance of fire-like sparks. This page transitions into the deeper state of "Beyond-Mind" (Unmani) and the vision of a pillar of light connecting the entire energy system.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 47

-> Like the form of a blazing lamp, and then following that, <-
-> a Light with the intense radiance of the rising sun, <-
-> which meets in the middle of the sky and the earth. <-

Because of this, it is said: "Therefore, this **Seal of the Sky-Walker** <term>Khechari Mudra</term> is the bestower of all perfections." In this state, because the consciousness "walks in the sky" of the inner space, it lacks any connection to the mind's usual activities. Because it is free from the knowledge of external objects, it becomes the **Beyond-Mind state** <term>Unmani</term>. <note>Khechari Mudra is a practice where the tongue is turned back into the nasal cavity, but here it also refers to the mental state of "moving in the void." Unmani is a state of samadhi where thought processes cease.</note>

Thus it has been said: "The Yogi who possesses the state **Beyond-Mind** is a true Yogi; there is no Yogi without this state." Elsewhere, the fact that the sacred syllable **Om** <term>Pranava</term> is surrounded by sparks of light is clearly stated: "Above that resides the Self in the form of the syllable Om, resembling a lamp, auspiciously surrounded on all sides by sparks of light like fireflies."

In what kind of place? In the **Abode of Great Bliss**, a place where happiness is uninterrupted. What is this "city" like? It is **Unsupported** <term>Niralamba</term>, meaning the mind's reliance on and connection to external sense-objects has vanished.

Furthermore, the text says this is **well-known through service to the Supreme Guru**. The "Supreme Guru" is one who is expert in the yoga practices passed down through the lineage. The meaning is that this state becomes fully understood through the instructions and the grace born from serving such a Guru. As it is said: "This is known only through the Guru's instruction, and not through millions of scriptures." What are the "sparks" like? They are **brightly shining**, appearing in a most excellent and luminous form. || 36 ||

The author now explains that for such Yogis, there is also the vision of another variety of fire-particles:

**Like a blazing lamp...** This means that following the vision of the fire-sparks, another vision occurs—the Yogi perceives a Light. He describes this Light as having the form of a blazing lamp... **meeting in the middle of the sky and the earth.** "Sky" <note>original: gagana</note> refers to the void-space at the peak where the **Conch-Channel** <term>Shankhini Nadi</term> resides. "Earth" <note>original: dharani</note> refers to the Earth-sphere located in the **Root Center** <term>Muladhara</term>. The Light is "meeting in the middle" of these two, meaning it pervades the entire space from the Root Center all the way up to the **Thousand-Petaled Lotus** <term>Sahasrara</term>.

<summary>This page describes the transition from seeing internal sparks to experiencing the "Beyond-Mind" state (Unmani) and perceiving a pillar of light that connects the Root Center to the crown of the head.</summary>
<keywords>Khechari Mudra, Unmani, Guru, Niralamba, Shankhini Nadi, Muladhara, Sahasrara, pillar of light</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the Ajna Chakra, specifically focusing on the presence of the Supreme Being (Parama Shiva) within this energy center. The previous page discussed the mental state of "Beyond-Mind" and the visual manifestation of the sacred syllable Om. This page identifies the specific deity presiding over this region and the mystical anatomy surrounding him.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 48

### The Presence of the Supreme Lord

> In this place, the Lord Himself is directly present, possessing complete glory and being undecaying;
> He is the Witness, as if within a circle composed of Fire, the Moon, and the Sun. ॥ 37 ॥

The phrase "in this place" provides the specific meaning. The word "or" in the previous context acts as a conjunction. Here, the text describes the presence of **Supreme Shiva** <term>Parama Shiva</term> in the **Command Circle** <term>Ajna Chakra</term>, just as he exists in the **Thousand-Petal Lotus** <term>Sahasrara</term>. The phrase "The Lord Himself is directly present" means that He exists here in His own person.

The text qualifies the Lord as having **Complete Glory** <term>Purna-vibhava</term>. This means He is complete within His own Self, and His "glory" consists of the power to act as the creator and to facilitate the union of all things. 

Alternatively, "glory" <term>vibhava</term> refers to the entire collection of the infinite manifested creation; He is "complete" because He exists as the all-pervading essence of that glory. This aligns with the Sacred Scripture <term>Shruti</term> which states: "From whom all these beings are born, by whom they live when born, and into whom they enter upon departing." <note>This is a famous quote from the Taittiriya Upanishad 3.1.1, defining the ultimate source of reality.</note> 

Or, "glory" means "all-pervasiveness" <term>vibhutvam</term>, and "complete" means He exists everywhere. In a more hidden sense, "completeness" refers to a state of will or desire that is not limited by external objects or the pursuit of specific results. The distinction here is that while other things—like space—are said to be all-pervading, they are not eternal in the same way the Self is.

### The Inner Vision according to the Nirvana Tantra

The presence of Supreme Shiva is specifically explained in the **Tantra of Liberation** <term>Nirvana Tantra</term>: 

"Above this lotus [the Ajna] is the **Lotus of Knowledge** <term>Jnana Padma</term>, which is very difficult to attain. It is joined with two petals and contains the circle of the full moon. Similarly, the seed-syllable of the Auspicious One <term>Shambhu-bija</term> is in its center, taking the form of the **Swan** <term>Hamsa</term>. This Swan resides in the **Jewel Island** <term>Mani-dvipa</term>, and in its lap is Supreme Shiva. On His left side is **Siddha-Kali** <note>A form of the Divine Mother representing perfected power</note>, whose very nature is eternal bliss."

Regarding the phrase "the Swan in its lap," <unclear>here</unclear> it refers to the space between the two dots that form the **Double-Dot** <term>Visarga</term>. The text says this while introducing the Thousand-Petal Lotus: "The two dots in the center take the form of the undecaying Visarga. In the middle of that, in the space of the Void, is Shiva, known as the Supreme." Since this vision is seen in the Sahasrara, it is sought here as well...

<vocab>षट्चक्रनिरूपण (Shat-Chakra-Nirupana: Investigation into the Six Chakras), आज्ञाचक्र (Ajna Chakra: The Command Center/Third Eye), परमशिव (Parama Shiva: The Supreme Shiva), निर्वाणतन्त्र (Nirvana Tantra: A foundational yogic text), हंस (Hamsa: The Swan, symbolizing the soul or breath), विसर्ग (Visarga: A Sanskrit punctuation mark of two dots, representing manifestation), विन्दु (Bindu: The point or seed of consciousness), मणिद्वीप (Mani-dvipa: The mythical Jewel Island of the heart/mind), पूर्णविभव (Purna-vibhava: Complete Glory/Power)</vocab>

It cites the Nirvana Tantra to map the mystical anatomy of the "Knowledge Lotus" and the "Jewel Island" residing within the practitioner's consciousness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the Ajna Chakra (the Third Eye center). # Investigation into the Six Chakras 48

> In this place of Vishnu, sweet with incomparable and supreme bliss, having placed the breath with a joyful mind at the time of death... the Supreme...

This refers to the state of things here. In this place, the union of **Shiva** and **Shakti** <term>the masculine and feminine divine principles</term> is hidden by the bonds of illusion <term>Maya</term>. They are to be understood as existing in the form of a **Supreme Point** <term>Bindu</term>, which is shaped like a **grain** <term>chanaka-akara</term>.
<note>The 'grain' or chickpea metaphor is significant in Tantric physiology; a chickpea has two halves (representing Shiva and Shakti) held together by a single skin (representing the power of Maya or illusion). It symbolizes the potential for all creation stored in a single point.</note>

Regarding the **Command Circle** <term>Ajna Chakra</term>, it has been said:

> According to the Utkala school and others, the Eternal Mother, appearing in the form of a grain, remains here and creates all living beings.

In this place, **Supreme Shiva** <term>Parama Shiva</term> always exists in this grain-like form. The text gives an example: "like fire," etc. This means that just as the Lord exists within the circles of the Sun, the Moon, and Fire, He exists here as well. The dwelling of the Lord within the celestial circles is a well-known spiritual truth.

Alternatively, it means that just as the Lord exists in the grain-like form within the circles of the Sun, Moon, and Fire located in the **Thousand-Petal Lotus** <term>Sahasrara</term>, so He exists here too. This manifestation in the thousand-petaled lotus will be shown later in the text.

Because of this, during the **Altar Worship** <term>Pitha-puja</term>, worship is prescribed for the **Supreme Self** <term>Paramatma</term> and the **Self of Knowledge** <term>Jnanatma</term> above the circles of the Sun, Moon, and Fire. One should meditate on the **Point** <term>Bindu</term> which is shaped like a grain and is composed of Shiva and Shakti—the Supreme Self being Shiva and the Self of Knowledge being his power of Wisdom—who are inseparable. ॥ 37 ॥

The text now describes the fruit of leaving the body through yoga at the Ajna Chakra: "In this place..."

The meaning is as follows: The Great Yogi <term>Yogindra</term>, at the time of **death** <term>prana-nidhane - literally 'the ending of the breath'</term>, becomes joyful in mind, his heart gladdened by the bliss of the Self. He places his life-breath <term>Prana</term> in this location—the **Command Circle** <term>Ajna Chakra</term>, which is the place of the Lord **Vishnu**, manifested in the form of the Point previously described. By placing the breath in that Point at the moment of death, he enters the **Supreme Person** <term>Parama Purusha</term>.

The text further describes this Supreme Person: He is **Eternal** <term>Nitya</term> and undecaying; He is the **Divine** <term>Deva</term> who plays through the acts of creation, preservation, and destruction; He is **Unborn** <term>Aja</term>, and the **Primal Source** of the three worlds. 

<note>original: "५" (Signature 5)</note>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 50

> The King of Yogis <term>Yogindra</term> clearly perceives that eternal God, the Primeval Person, unborn, the first cause of the three worlds, the Ancient One who is known through the **Vedanta** <term>the philosophical conclusion of the Vedas</term>. || 38 ||

By being "prior to all," His status as the universal cause is indicated. He is called "Ancient" because he is the **Ancient Person** <note>original: "Purana-Purusha," a title for the supreme being as the oldest of all</note>. The term **Vedanta** refers to the Upanishadic scriptures that define **Brahman** <term>the Absolute Reality</term>; the meaning is that He is the one to be known through that specific knowledge.

Now, this is the method for placing the life-breath <term>Prana</term> in the "Place of Vishnu" <note>This refers to the Ajna Chakra where the yogi's consciousness is concentrated</note>. Recognizing the approaching time of death, the practitioner—becoming absorbed in Brahman with a joyful mind—sits in a yoga posture. By restraining the breath through **Kumbhaka** <term>breath retention</term>, they bring the individual soul <term>Jivatma</term> situated in the heart down to the **Root Center** <term>Muladhara</term>. Then, by contracting the anus <note>a technique known as Mula Bandha</note> and raising the **Kundalini** <term>the serpent power or spiritual energy</term> according to the prescribed method, they meditate on the **Sound** <term>Nada</term>. 

This Sound is the very essence of Kundalini, shaped like a thread, blissful, and appearing like a flash of lightning stretching from the root all the way to the **Opening of Brahman** <term>Brahmarandhra; the crown of the head</term>. The yogi then dissolves the **Hamsa** <note>literally "Swan," representing the breath and the soul</note>, which is the Supreme Self in the form of the breath, into that Sound. Along with the individual soul, they bring this energy up through the sequence of piercing the chakras until it reaches the **Command Center** <term>Ajna Chakra</term>. 

Within the Kundalini situated there, they dissolve the entire created universe—the elements starting with Earth—proceeding from the gross to the subtle. They then unify that energy, along with the individual soul, with the **Point** <term>Bindu</term> located there, which consists of the union of **Shiva and Shakti**. The yogi should remain in this state. Finally, by piercing the Opening of Brahman and leaving the body, they become fully absorbed into the Absolute.

This, then, is the determination of the meaning of the **Great Sayings** <term>Mahavakyas; such as "I am That"</term>.

The **Command Center** <term>Ajna Chakra</term> has two petals. It is white in color, and its two petals bear the letters **Ha** and **Ksha**, which are of a variegated or grayish hue. In the center <term>Karnika</term> dwells the presiding goddess of the chakra, **Hakini Shakti**. She is white in color, with six red faces, three eyes, and six arms. She holds the gestures of granting boons and dispelling fear <note>Vara and Abhaya mudras</note>, as well as a rosary, a skull-cup, a small drum <term>Damaru</term>, and a book. She is seated upon a white lotus.

Above her, within a triangle, is the **Other Linga** <term>Itara-linga</term>, which is white and possesses the brilliance of lightning. In the triangle above that is the **Inner Soul** <term>Antaratma</term> in the shape of the **Om** symbol <term>Pranava</term>, glowing like a lamp. In the space surrounding it in all four directions, it is encircled by spheres of sparks of light. By its own radiance, which resembles a blazing lamp, it illuminates everything from the Root Center to the Opening of Brahman. Above that...

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 51

Above that <note>the Bindu or Divine Point</note> is the place where the wind dissolves <note>original: "Vayu-laya-sthanam"</note>. Above it is the form of the **Great Sound** <term>Mahanada</term>, which is half of Shiva <note>representing the union of the masculine and feminine divine</note>, shaped like a vein, peaceful, the giver of boons and the remover of fear, and the light of pure intelligence. When a yogi—who has become virtuous through the service of the lotus-like feet of his Guru—perceives this, then the mastery of speech <term>Vacham Siddhi</term> shall always reside in the palm of his hand. || 36 ||

---

The mind is of a subtle form. Above that, in the Lunar Circle <term>Chandra-mandala</term>, is the **Swan** <term>Hamsa</term>, who is the Supreme Shiva accompanied by His Power <term>Shakti</term>. || 38 ||

---

Now, desiring to describe the intermediate causal body <term>Karana-sharira</term> situated above the **Ajna Chakra** and below the **Thousand-Petaled Lotus** <term>Sahasrara</term>, the author says: "the place where the wind dissolves." 

The meaning is this: when a yogi—who is "virtuous through service to the Guru’s lotus feet" (meaning his conduct is beautiful and his yogic practice is perfected through firm, one-pointed concentration on the Guru)—sees the form of the **Great Sound** <term>Mahanada</term> above the Ajna Chakra, then for that yogi, at all times, the "Mastery of Speech" <note>the supernatural power where whatever one speaks becomes reality</note> will surely manifest in the palm of his hand. 

The author further describes this Sound as the "place of absorption for the wind." In this Great Sound, the wind <term>Vayu</term> is dissolved. This follows the rule that a thing dissolves back into that from which it was produced. Although in the practice of "elemental purification" <term>Bhuta-shuddhi</term>, the wind is usually seen to dissolve into the element of touch or into space, it is also the case that the wind dissolves into the Sound <term>Nada</term>. 

Regarding this, from the letter **A** was produced the Earth <note>described as 'possessing taste'</note>; from the letter **Ka** were produced all the waters and holy rivers; from the letter **Ra** came the principle of fire; from **Sound** <term>Nada</term> came the Wind/Air, which is the essence of all life-force <term>Prana</term>; and from the **Point** <term>Bindu</term> came Space <term>Gagana</term>, which is the Great Void and the essence of sound. From these arise the principles composed of the twenty-five qualities <note>the Tattvas, or fundamental building blocks of reality in Indian philosophy</note>.

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 52

Above that, at the peak of the *Shankhini* <note>a specific psychic nerve or channel</note>, in the space of the void, shines the lotus of a thousand petals, which is below the *Visarga* <note>the terminal point of manifestation</note> and is more brilliant than the full moon—

---

"All this universe, this entire cosmos, is composed of the Goddess Kali," according to the words of the *Shruti* <note>sacred revealed scripture</note>. It should be meditated upon that during the subtle stage of dissolution <note>original: "samharana-kale"</note> of the letters of the Kali-mantra, the element of Wind <term>Vayu</term> dissolves into the **Sound** <term>Nada</term>. 

Regarding the phrase "half of Shiva" <note>original: "shivardham"</note>: because Shiva exists in the form of the **Lord Who is Half-Woman** <term>Ardhanarishvara</term>, His "half" is **Shakti** <note>the Divine Feminine Power</note>; thus, the Sound is of that very nature. 

Regarding the phrase "shaped like a vein" <note>original: "sirakaram"</note>: starting with the dental "s," this refers to the shape of a vein or a plow <term>langala</term>. This is supported by the dialogue between Lord Kartikeya and Ishvara <note>Shiva</term>: "Above that is the **Great Sound** <term>Mahanada</term>, shining and shaped like a plow." 

Even though the *Amarakosha* <note>a famous Sanskrit dictionary</note> uses the fourth vowel <note>the long 'i' in 'seera'</note> to denote a plow, here, following the consistency of other sacred texts, the word *sira* with the third vowel <note>the short 'i'</note> is also understood to mean a plow. 

Alternatively, if the word is read as *shivakaram* <note>starting with the palatal 'sh'</note>, it means this Sound consists of the union of Shiva and Shakti. As stated in the *Prapanchasara*: "Awakened as the nature of Sound, She <note>Shakti</note> is inclined toward the state beyond all illness <note>liberation</note>. When Shakti is inclined toward Shiva, She is then remembered in a masculine form." 

For this reason, the scholar Raghava Bhatta also stated that Sound <term>Nada</term> and the Point <term>Bindu</term> are manifestations of that very Shakti. Elsewhere it is said: "That which is known as Continuity, which is pure Consciousness, due to its proximity to the Light and its desire to create, becomes dense and in some places attains the state of the Point." 

Likewise, the Holy Teacher <note>referring to Shankaracharya</note> has said: "Sound itself, having become dense, in some places attains the state of the Point." By examining all these statements, the conclusion is that Shakti herself appears in the form of Sound and the Point, just as gold appears in the form of a gold earring <note>a classic metaphor: the underlying substance remains the same despite the change in shape</note>. The Sound and the Point are essentially of one form. The text concludes by calling it the "giver of boons." || 39 ||

---

Now, for those practitioners who cultivate yoga through meditative absorption <term>Samadhi</term>, from the time of that absorption...

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on the transition from the lower chakras to the Sahasrara (the Thousand-Petaled Lotus). The previous page discussed the nature of the "Great Sound" (Mahanada) and the dissolution of elements. This page begins with Verse 40, describing the appearance of the Sahasrara, and then provides a detailed philosophical breakdown of the gross and subtle elements (tattvas) that a practitioner must mentally "dissolve" during meditation.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 53

->White. Facing downwards, beautiful, with the splendor of a sliver of the rising sun; its cluster of filaments is adorned with the letters starting from the forehead <note>original: "lalaata-adyaih"; this refers to the 50 letters of the Sanskrit alphabet which are repeated to populate the 1,000 petals</note>; its body is the essence of pure bliss. || 40 ||<-

Following the instruction of the teacher—"Having previously meditated thus with great effort, one should dissolve everything into the **Soul of Consciousness** <term>Chidatman</term> through the sequence of gross and subtle forms"—it is necessary to understand these forms. Because the scriptures prescribe contemplating the entire collection of the created world in its gross and subtle aspects before entering the state of deep absorption <term>Samadhi</term>, that knowledge is provided here.

First, the five **Gross Forms** <term>Sthula-rupa</term> (Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether/Space) have been described in the chakras from the **Root Center** <term>Muladhara</term> to the **Purity Center** <term>Vishuddha</term>. Because they are situated within those centers, they are categorized as follows:

| Center / Element | Associated Faculties |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Earth Region** (Muladhara) | The feet (action), the nose (senses), and the essence of smell. |
| **Water Region** | The hands (action), the tongue (senses), and the essence of taste. |
| **Fire Region** | The anus (action), the eyes (senses), and the essence of form/sight. |
| **Air Region** | The genitals (action), the skin (senses), and the essence of touch. |
| **Space Region** (Vishuddha) | The voice (action), the ears (senses), and the essence of sound. |

In total, these constitute fifteen principles <term>tattvas</term>. When combined with the five primary elements (Earth, etc.), there are twenty **Gross Principles** to be understood.

Now, the **Subtle Forms** <term>Sukshma-rupa</term> are established. In the **Command Center** <term>Ajna Chakra</term>, the subtle form of the **Mind** <term>Manas</term> has already been shown. Furthermore, the *Kankalamalini Tantra* says regarding the Ajna Chakra:

"The Mind always shines here, colored by the power of the goddess **Hakini Shakti**. It is the supreme luminous state, adorned with **Intellect** <term>Buddhi</term>, **Nature** <term>Prakriti</term>, and **Ego** <term>Ahankara</term>."

From this, the presence of the three subtle principles—Intellect, Nature, and Ego—is understood to be located here. Regarding the **Ego** <term>Ahankara</term>, its position is not determined by the <unclear>textual</unclear> sequence. Rather, because the Ego is the generator of Earth and the other elements, and is the foundation into which they dissolve, it is observed that the cause (the generator) must stand below that which is dissolved into it. Since the logic of the sequence of sound is more authoritative than the sequence of the written text, its position is conceptualized in that way. Therefore, "In the Ego... the Space with sound... and in the Great Principle <term>Mahat</term> also..." <note>The text here becomes fragmentary due to damage, but refers to the process of merging individual consciousness into universal principles.</note>

<summary>This page defines the 20 gross elements (tattvas) associated with the lower five chakras and the subtle elements (Mind, Intellect, Nature, Ego) located in the Ajna Chakra. It explains the necessity of understanding these elements to perform the "dissolution" required for Samadhi.</summary>

<keywords>Sahasrara, Tattvas, Gross Elements, Subtle Elements, Ajna Chakra, Hakini Shakti, Samadhi, Ego, Intellect, Kankalamalini Tantra</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from page 53 regarding the dissolution of the elements (tattvas) during meditation. While the previous page categorized the gross elements (Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether) and their sensory associations within the lower five chakras, this page shifts focus to the "Command Center" (Ajna Chakra) and the subtle psychic principles that reside above it, specifically detailing the hierarchy of the Mind, Ego, Intellect, and Primordial Nature.</meta>

# Investigation into the Chakras 54

Afterward, for the purpose of dissolving **Ether** <term>Vyoma</term>, one encounters the **Ego** <term>Ahamkara</term> first, as it is the foundation where Ether dissolves. Therefore, the Ego is situated first, and above it lies the position of the **Intellect** <term>Buddhi</term> and **Nature** <term>Prakriti</term>. 

The *Sharada-Tilaka* explains the cause-and-effect relationship between these principles: "From that fundamental, unmanifest, and modified Supreme Reality arose the **Great Principle** <term>Mahat-tattva</term>, which consists of the three qualities and the inner instrument. From that arose the Ego in its three forms, according to the different stages of creation." <note>original: "mūlabhūtāt-tato'vyaktāt..."</note> By "modified" <term>vikrita</term>, it is meant that this principle is a reflection of the Supreme Reality, taking on a modified form; it is also called **Nature** <term>Prakriti</term> because it serves as the source-material for the Great Principle and the others.

Thus it has been said: "Nature is the Supreme Power, and its modification is its reflection." Likewise, it was shown previously that the Supreme Brahman reflects its own nature. 

Regarding the mention of the **Great Principle** <term>Mahat-tattva</term> in the *Sharada-Tilaka* text: that which is the Great Principle is indeed the **Intellect** <term>Buddhi</term>. As stated by the author Ishanashiva: "That very Nature, expanded by Power <term>Shakti</term>, possesses the characteristic of being knowable. The principle of Intellect becomes manifest, relying on the quality of **Purity** <term>Sattva</term>. That same Intellect is celebrated in the Samkhya philosophy as the 'Great' principle." 

The Great Principle, however, consists of the three qualities and the **Inner Instrument** <term>Antahkarana</term>. The qualities consist of **Purity, Passion, and Inertia** <term>Sattva, Rajas, Tamas</term>. Regarding the Inner Instrument, the *Sharada-Tilaka* states: "The Inner Instrument of the Soul is proclaimed to be the **Mind**, **Intellect**, **Ego**, and **Consciousness** <term>Manas, Buddhi, Ahamkara, Chitta</term>." The Great Principle is the collective assembly of these.

One might ask: "If the Mind is already included within the Great Principle, how is it consistent to mention it separately in the description of this chakra, where it is said 'the Mind shines here in its subtle form'?" 

The answer is: "No, that Mind [mentioned in the chakra] is not the one contained within the Great Principle, but rather a distinct Mind produced from the Ego." As the commentator Raghava Bhatta explains: "Because the other principle of Mind—the one characterized by resolve and doubt—is known to arise specifically from the **Fiery Ego** <term>Taijasa-Ahamkara</term>."

Thus, since the sequence has been established for everything up to the Mind situated in the **Command Center** <term>Ajna Chakra</term>, the positions of the Ego and subsequent principles above it should be understood. Consequently, in the Command Center, there is a direct, vertical sequence of the Goddess **Hakini**, the **Other-Linga** <term>Itara-linga</term>, the **Sacred Syllable** <term>Pranava</term>, the **Mind**, the **Ego**, the **Intellect**, and **Nature**...

<summary>This page defines the hierarchy of subtle elements (Ego, Intellect, and Nature) located above the Ajna Chakra, clarifying that the "Mind" experienced in meditation is an evolution of the Ego rather than the cosmic Mahat-tattva. It reconciles technical differences between Samkhya philosophy and Tantric practice regarding the "Inner Instrument."</summary>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 55

Because the sequence of positions has been established, the location of the **Moon-sphere** <term>Chandra-mandala</term>—which was shown previously—must be situated above them. If one asks, "Why is it not placed below them?" the answer is no. According to the words of the *Sammohana Tantra*: "The Moon is in the forehead region, and above that is Bodhini herself." This indicates the position of the **Moon** <term>Indu</term> and **Bodhini** <term>the Awakener</term>, which reside above the **Command Center** <term>Ajna Chakra</term> described hereafter. To ensure there is no obstruction between them, its position is at the very top. As the *Sammohana Tantra* describes the causal forms above the Command Center:

> The **Moon** <term>Indu</term> is in the forehead region, and above that is **Bodhini** <term>the Awakener</term> herself. Above that shines the **Sound** <term>Nada</term>, supreme and shaped like a half-moon. Above that is the **Great Sound** <term>Mahanada</term>, brilliant and shaped like a plow. Above that is the **Digit** <term>Kala</term> called **Anji**, dear to yogis. Above that is the **Mindless State** <term>Unmani</term>, reaching which one does not return. <note>The "Mindless State" refers to the highest state of meditation where the individual mind dissolves into pure consciousness.</note>

Regarding the phrase "above that is Bodhini," this means above the forehead, or rather, above the Command Center. The *Bhuta-shuddhi Tantra* describes the position of the **Drop** <term>Bindu</term> below Bodhini:

> The **Drop** <term>Bindu</term> arises from the **Half-measure** <term>matrardha</term>, O Goddess; above that is the state called **Sound** <term>Nada</term>. Above that is the place of dissolution for the vital air, the **Great Sound** <term>Mahanada</term>.

The "Half-measure" refers to **Power** <term>Shakti</term>. 

> The **Drop-syllable** <term>Bindumad-akshara</term> appeared like the morning sun. Above that is the **Half-measure** <term>Ardhamatra</term>, residing in the Gandhara musical note. <note>original: "bālasūryapratīkāśam..."</note>

Based on this statement from the *Brihat-Trivikrama-Samhita*, it is understood that the Half-measure is a synonym for Power. By harmonizing these two texts, one should understand that the "Half-measure" is indeed **Bodhini**. This triad of Bindu, Bodhini, and Nada is a specific manifestation of the **Supreme Power** <term>Para-Shakti</term> composed of the Drop. As stated in the *Sharada-Tilaka*:

> From the Supreme Lord, who is the abundance of **Existence, Consciousness, and Bliss** <term>Saccidananda</term> and possesses all aspects <unclear>sakala</unclear>, arose **Power** <term>Shakti</term>. From Power came **Sound** <term>Nada</term>, and from Sound, the **Drop** <term>Bindu</term> was born. This Power, consisting of the Supreme, manifests visibly in three ways. Its divisions are proclaimed as the **Drop** <term>Bindu</term>, the **Sound** <term>Nada</term>, and the **Seed** <term>Bija</term>. The Drop is of the nature of Shiva, the Seed is Power, and Sound is the relationship between them. It is called the inherence of all—

It reconciles various Tantric sources to define the relationship between Shakti, Nada, and Bindu.</summary>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 56

...by those expert in the scriptures. The **Drop** <term>Bindu</term> is composed of the Supreme Power <term>Parashakti</term> and the Supreme Shiva. This Drop, situated above the forehead, is of the nature of **Sound** <term>Nada</term> and of Shiva. The **Seed** <term>Bija</term> is the **Awakening Power** <term>Bodhini-rupa</term>. The Sound is the perpetual union or relationship between these two, acting as the "disturber and the disturbed"; thus, it takes the form of the **Power of Action** <term>Kriya-shakti</term>. 

Above these three is the **Great Sound** <term>Mahanada</term> previously shown. Above that, the **Digit** <term>Kala</term> is described. The Digit is the Power called **Anji**, which takes the form of a slanted or curved line <note>original: "tiyak-rekha." This refers to a specific calligraphic mark in Sanskrit used to represent a subtle vibration of sound.</note>. This Power appeared at the beginning of creation, as stated in the *Pancharatra*:

> "Looking thus at the beginning of creation, the Supreme Person makes manifest that eternal **Nature** <term>Prakriti</term>, who consists of Being, Consciousness, and Bliss <term>Sacchidananda</term>, who contains the assembly of all principles, and who is the presiding deity of memory."

Elsewhere it is said:

> "From the union of Shiva and Shakti, from the unmanifest Supreme Lord, the primordial Goddess appeared; she is indeed **Tripura Sundari** <term>The Beautiful One of the Three Cities</term>. From Power <term>Shakti</term> came Sound <term>Nada</term>, and from Sound, the Drop <term>Bindu</term> was born."

Regarding the **Mindless State** <term>Unmani</term> which is above that, its characteristics are described:

> "That state is called **Mindless** <term>Unmani</term> in all the secret scriptures where, upon reaching it, the mind’s very 'mind-ness' no longer exists." <note>This refers to the dissolution of the egoic, thinking mind into pure, undifferentiated awareness.</note>

Therefore, the essence of the Mindless State is the achievement of a total absence of mental activity—the state where the mind no longer seeks to grasp external objects. This state is two-fold: it resides in the **Thousand-Petaled Lotus** <term>Sahasrara</term> and takes the form of the **Digit of Liberation** <term>Nirvana-kala</term>, and in this location, it consists of the **String of Letters** <term>Varnavali</term>. 

Thus, in the *Kankalamalini Tantra*:

> "In the pericarp of the Thousand-Petaled Lotus, in the middle of the Moon-sphere, is the **Seventeenth Digit** <term>Kala</term> which is free from all thoughts and intentions. Its name is indeed **Unmani**, and it cuts the bonds of worldly existence."

Furthermore, because of the "garland of letters" in the Mindless state, it grants liberation. "Garland of letters" means it is composed of the totality of all sounds/phonemes.

Below the Mindless state is the **Balanced State** <term>Samani</term>, as described in the *Bhuta-shuddhi*:

> "From that comes the Pervasive Power which people know as **Anji**. Above that is **Samani**, and above her is **Unmani**..."

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 57

->**The Hierarchy of Subtle Powers**<-

"Let us bring that above..." and so forth. This refers to a secondary form of the **Supreme Power** <term>Parashakti</term>. Above the **Command Center** <term>Ajna Chakra</term> sits the second **Drop** <term>Bindu</term>, which is of the nature of Shiva. Above that is the **Awakening Power** <term>Bodhini Shakti</term> in the form of a half-measure <note>original: "ardha-matra," a term from Sanskrit prosody referring to a subtle unit of time in sound</note>. Above that is **Sound** <term>Nada</term>, shaped like a half-moon and representing the union of Shiva and Shakti. Above that is the **Great Sound** <term>Mahanada</term>, shaped like a plow. Above that is the **Pervasive Power** <term>Vyapika Shakti</term> in the form of an *Anji* <note>a specific hooked calligraphic mark</note>. Above that is **Shamani** <note>The "Queller" of thoughts</note>, and above that is **Unmani** <note>The "Mindless" or Transcendent state</note>. These exist in sequence as the seven causal forms.

Enough with excessive detail; let us return to the main subject. The author describes the **Thousand-Petaled Lotus** <term>Sahasradala Padma</term> with ten verses, starting with the phrase "Above that."

**Location of the Sahasrara**

"Above that"—meaning above everything previously shown—at the peak or head of the **Shankhini Nadi** <term>A subtle energy channel located in the head</term>, in the region of the void, a place not covered by any Nadi. By this, it is indicated that it is even above the **Sushumna** <term>The central energy channel of the spine</term>.

It is situated below the **Visarga** <term>A pair of dots in Sanskrit representing a breathy release; here a mystical point of energy</term>. The Thousand-Petaled Lotus resides there. The Visarga is in the upper part of the **Aperture of Brahma** <term>Brahmarandhra</term>. In that aperture, the Visarga is associated with "Eternal Bliss" and the "Stainless One," according to verses that will be quoted later.

**The Petals and the Letters**

The author describes the Lotus as having a body shining with the letters of the alphabet, beginning with the letter **Na**. Here, the letter **Na** is mentioned not because we should meditate on the letters in reverse order, but rather to exclude the letter **Ksha**. 

The presence of the letters on the Thousand-Petaled Lotus occurs through repetition. If one includes the letter **Ksha**, there are fifty-one letters. Repeating these twenty times would result in 1020 petals. If one repeated them nineteen times, there would be only 969 petals. Therefore, by excluding the letter **Ksha**, the count is resolved. We should understand that the fifty letters from **A** to **La** are to be meditated upon in their natural order, repeating the cycle twenty times to fill the thousand petals.

This meditation on the letters in their natural order is clearly stated in the *Tantra of the Garland of Skulls*:

> "The Sahasrara is the Great Lotus, white in color, with its head hanging downward. It is adorned with the shimmering letters from **A** to **Ksha**." 

<margin>The author notes that "ending with Ksha" in the quoted Tantra may be interpreted differently in other contexts to maintain the thousand-fold count.</margin>

<summary>This page describes the subtle energy levels (Nada, Bodhini, etc.) that bridge the Ajna Chakra to the Sahasrara and explains the mathematical logic of assigning the Sanskrit alphabet to the lotus's 1000 petals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the description of the Sahasrara (Thousand-Petaled Lotus) at the crown of the head. It provides a technical commentary on the arrangement of the Sanskrit alphabet (Matrika) within the petals of this lotus and addresses a grammatical debate concerning the gender of the word "lotus" in the source verses. It bridges the transition from the general structure of the Sahasrara to the specific visualization of the Moon within its center.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 58

->Inside it resides the **Moon**, free from the hare <note>The "hare" refers to the shadows on the moon; a moon without the hare is a symbol of absolute, stainless purity</note>, pure and full, casting a net of shimmering beams, glistening with the mass of supreme nectar.<-

Because the word "end" <term>avasana</term> signifies a conclusion, the phrase "ending with the letter *Ksa*" <note>original: "ksakaro'nte"</note> uses a compound form to indicate the fifty letters of the alphabet ending with *La*, excluding *Ksa*. Alternatively, it may refer to the fifty letters starting with *La* and ending with *Ksa* which are bound together in a circular arrangement <note>original: "akatha-di-vinyasah"</note> like a garland.

The *Kankalamalini* <note>A classic Tantric text centered on the Goddess</note> teaches that the arrangement of these letters should be clockwise; therefore, there is no contradiction between these two views. Since the specific colors of these fifty letters are not explicitly mentioned here, and since the "Mother" alphabet letters <term>Matrika</term> are traditionally seen as white, they should be meditated upon here as being white in color.

However, if one follows the reading "the letters beginning with the forehead" <note>original: "lalatadyair-varnair"</note>, then the word "forehead" refers to the letter **A**, which is the first letter assigned to the forehead in the ritual of placing the alphabet on the body <term>Matrika-nyasa</term>. In this interpretation, the fifty letters are understood to run from **A** to **Ksa**, excluding the letter **La**. This is because, in almost all systems using fifty letters, the final "n" <note>the Vedic 'L' or 'N'</note> is omitted.

On this point, some scholars prefer the reading "of glistening body" <note>original: "pravilasitatana"</note>. They argue that since the word "lotus" <term>padma</term> is occasionally treated as a masculine noun, the adjective "body" <term>tanu</term> should also take the masculine form. We disagree. In this verse, the word "lotus" is written with a terminal dot <note>the *anusvara* or "m" sound</note>, clearly indicating it is being used in the neuter gender. If it were masculine, it would end with a breathy aspiration <note>original: "visarga," the "h" sound</note>. Therefore, its adjective "body" must also be neuter. To read it otherwise would break the poetic meter of the verse. The rest of the passage is self-explanatory.

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 56

->...shining with a continuous radiance. Within it [the Moon] flashes a **Triangle**, shaped like a lightning bolt; within that is the **Void**, served by all the gods and kept very secret. — Verse 41<-

The text says, "it resides" <note>original: "maste"</note>. "Inside it" <note>original: "tatra-antah"</note> refers to the center of that Lotus. It is "pure," meaning it is characterized by a stainless rising. 

The author describes the **Moon** with the phrase "shining," and so on. "Shining with a continuous radiance <note>original: "santana-hasi"</note>, glistening with the mass of supreme nectar." The word "glistening" <term>snigdha</term> implies a state like moisture or tenderness, and "continuous" <term>santana</term> refers to its expansion. Therefore, the phrase means it is characterized by rays full of bliss, resembling a smile joined with a glistening expansion, because it is free from heat due to the mass of supreme nectar.

Now, regarding the arrangement of the **Inner Self** <term>Antaratma</term> and the others below and above the Moon-orb, the *Kankalamalini Tantra* <note>A significant text in the Shakta tradition that details the anatomy of the subtle body</note> states:

> Starting with the **Thousand-Petaled Lotus** <term>Sahasrara</term>, within its pericarp <note>The seed-vessel or center of the lotus</note>, O Goddess, is the **Inner Self**, and then the **Guru**. Then the **Sun-orb** and the **Moon-orb** themselves. Then the great **Wind** <term>Vayu</term>, and then the **Cavity of Brahma** <term>Brahmarandhra</term> is remembered. In that cavity is the **Creative Emission** <term>Visarga</term>, which is eternal bliss and stainless. Above that is the Goddess **Shankhini**, the cause of creation, preservation, and destruction.

"Inside it" means that in the middle of the Moon-orb, a **Triangle** shaped like a lightning bolt constantly flashes. Regarding the phrase "the Void within it": "within it" means that which has a void in its center. This refers to the body of the **Supreme Point** <term>Param-Bindu</term>. Within that Triangle is the Void; thus, the "Void within it" refers to the Void in the form of the Supreme Point dwelling in the middle of the Triangle—this is the action described.

Alternatively, within that Triangle, the Void in the form of the Supreme Point flashes. Therefore it is said in the sixth chapter of the *Todala Tantra*:

> The Supreme Light is formless <term>Nirakara</term>, and the **Point** <term>Bindu</term> is called the Imperishable. By the word "Point," the **Void** <term>Shunya</term> is meant, and it also indicates the Qualities <term>Guna</term>.

Because of the teaching that "Here, the practices of intake, elimination, and recreation should be well-guarded by one who knows the Law <term>Dharma</term>," and because of the command to keep Yoga secret...

->It is well-concealed and [attained] with effort; it is the supreme root of the mass of the continuous stream of supreme bliss. Because it is not achieved through [mere] ritual performance, the **Void** <term>Shunya</term> within it is extremely hidden. It is "served" <note>original: "sevitam"</note>, meaning it is worshipped by the entire host of gods. — [Commentary on Verse] 41

Regarding the phrase **"Well-concealed"** <note>original: "suguptam"</note>: The meaning is as follows. Because it has the form of a single ten-millionth part of the end of a hair <note>A traditional Indian measurement for extreme subtlety</note>, this Void is very hidden. Through "effort"—meaning through the practice of long and continuous meditation—it manifests as the pure form of all the digits of the moon. 

The "digit" <term>kala</term> mentioned here is the **Digit of Liberation** <term>Nirvanakala</term>. The meaning is that its pure form is revealed by that sixteenth digit of the moon. Furthermore, even though it is extremely subtle, the Supreme Void—which exists within the **Triangle** <term>Trikona</term> and has the form of the **Point** <term>Bindu</term> along with the Digit of Liberation—becomes revealed through meditation. 

The phrase **"Supreme root of the mass of the continuous stream of supreme bliss"** means it is the primary root of **Liberation** <term>Moksha</term>. 

Regarding the word "well-concealed" <note>original: "suguptam"</note>, if the reading "to be well-protected" <note>original: "sugopyam"</note> is used, one should consider that the word "with effort" <note>original: "yatnat"</note> applies to both. 

Some, however, explain the "Void within the Triangle" as follows: What is the nature of that void-space? Its light is the pure form of all the digits of the moon. This means its light is like that of the full moon, possessing all sixteen digits of the moon in their pure, white form. But this interpretation should be considered carefully. By stating that this Triangle exists within the center of the full Moon-orb, it is already established that the space in the middle of the Triangle has a light equal to the full moon...

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 61

## Constantly discharging an excessive shower of nectar-streams...

If [this state] were obtained [easily], then stating it again would be redundant. Furthermore, by the phrase "served by all the hosts of gods," it is not appropriate to suggest that a "void" or empty space is the object of service. Rather, it is the **Point** <term>Bindu; the concentrated seed of energy</term> within the **Triangle** <note>The Kamakala triangle within the Sahasrara</note> that is to be served. If one argues that the "Void" is served because it is the dwelling place of the Supreme Point, the wise should consider that in such a case, it is not actually a "void" at all.

Regarding the presence of **Paramashiva** <note>The Supreme Shiva, representing the highest consciousness</note> in the pericarp of the thousand-petaled lotus, the text says: **"In this place."** This means within the **Pericarp** <term>Karnika; the center of the lotus flower</term>. The remaining sense is that the God known by the name **Paramashiva** resides here.

The text describes this God as:
**"Possessing His own form"** <note>original: "svarupi"</note>, which is to say He has the nature of **Brahman** <term>the ultimate, infinite reality</term>. 
**"The Self of all"** <note>original: "sarvatma"</note>, meaning the soul of all living beings, the nature of the individual soul. This is because, in absolute reality, there is no difference between the **Individual Soul** <term>Jivatma</term> and the **Supreme Soul** <term>Paramatma</term>. The Self is that very living soul. Thus, it is stated in the *Adhyatma Ramayana*:

> The individual soul and the supreme soul are synonyms; one should not perceive a difference here. 
> When the knowledge of unity arises through the instructions of the teacher and the scriptures, 
> then alone is the primal ignorance regarding the "two" souls—the individual and the supreme—[dissolved].

The **Scripture** <term>Shruti; the Vedas</term> also demonstrates the identity of the two terms through the famous sentence: **"That Thou Art"** <note>original: "Tat Tvam Asi," one of the Great Sayings (Mahavakyas) of the Upanishads</note>.

Regarding the phrase **"The tasted and the untasted"** <note>original: "rasavirasa-mitam"</note>: "Rasa" is the flavor of supreme bliss. "Virasa" is its opposite. Because of the **Harmony** <term>samarasya; the perfect equilibrium</term> born from the union of **Shiva and Shakti**, His nature is Bliss. "Mita" means this state has been attained. 

Alternatively, "Rasa" refers to the worldly flavors of sense-objects, as they are encountered first. The "untasted" or "intellect beyond flavor" refers to the realization that discards the limited nature of these objects. Through that realization, the flavor of supreme bliss is reached.

**"The Swan for the darkness of ignorance and delusion"**: Because He destroys the darkness of ignorance and delusion, He is the **Swan** <note>original: "Hamsa"; a symbol of the soul and the sun</note>, meaning He has the form of the Sun. — [Commentary on Verse] 42

**"Constantly discharging an excessive shower of nectar-streams..."**: [The word] "Constantly" [implies]...

The previous page established that Paramashiva (the Supreme Shiva) resides in the Sahasrara (the thousand-petaled lotus at the crown of the head) and is non-different from the individual soul. This page identifies Paramashiva as the Supreme Guru who grants liberation through the Taraka mantra.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 62

->To the <unclear>ascetic</unclear> of pure mind, the Lord grants knowledge of the Self. The Lord of all resides there, a flood of the continuous waves of all happiness, known by the name of the Supreme Swan. || 43 ||<-

**"...constantly discharging an excessive shower of nectar-streams..."** This means he is perpetually releasing the essential substance in the form of a stream of nectar. Alternatively, the moon is the "receptacle of nectar" <note>original: *sudhā-ādhāra*</note>; that which is released or manifested from it is the essence of the nectar-stream—the white-colored rays of immortality. By this description, it is suggested that the subject [Paramashiva] is white in color, like the moon.

Or, "discharging" can mean "uttering." In this sense, the "nectar-stream" refers to his speech; his words are full of nectar. Thus, at all times, to destroy the power of delusion, he utters powerful, nectar-like words. To the ascetic <term>Yati; one who has restrained the mind and senses</term> whose mind is fixed on their chosen deity, he grants **Knowledge of the Self** <note>original: *svātma-jñāna*</note>. This is the means by which the **Individual Soul** <term>Jivatma</term> is realized to be inseparable from the **Supreme Soul** <term>Paramatma</term>. The collective meaning is that he instructs the seeker in the **Taraka-Brahma-Mantra** <term>A "crossing" mantra that delivers the soul from the cycle of rebirth</term>.

Furthermore, by describing him as the teacher of this nectar-like Brahma-mantra which grants Self-knowledge, his status as the **Guru** <term>Spiritual Teacher</term> is indicated. This description applies because the Paramashiva mentioned in the previous verse is indeed the Guru. Therefore, in the *Lalita-Rahasya* <note>A sacred text detailing the mysteries of the Goddess Lalita Tripura Sundari</note>, in the section titled "Investigation into the Principle of the Guru" <note>original: *Guru-tattva-nirūpaṇe*</note>, the Guru is described: "He is the famous, best of men, who always delights in the Self." After this description, it continues: "His beloved <note>Referring to the power or Shakti of the Guru</note> is of the form of light; she is the path to the Absolute which is so difficult to reach. The Supreme Absolute <term>Brahman</term> itself is the glorious radiance of his lotus feet." By mentioning this excessive beauty of his lotus feet...

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary regarding the identity and location of the Supreme Guru within the Sahasrara (the thousand-petaled lotus). It specifically reconciles various descriptions from different Tantric texts to clarify that the Supreme Shiva and the Guru are one and the same, situated above the "Swan" (Hamsa) of the individual soul.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 63

It has been explained that he dwells within the heart-lotus of the **Supreme Shiva, whose form is the Supreme Brahma** <note>original: *parabrahma-rūpa-paramaśiva*</note>. The "climbing upon the two feet" of the **Beloved of the Guru** <note>original: *guru-priyā*; likely referring to the power or Shakti associated with the Guru</note>, who is of the nature of light, occurs only within the heart of the Guru and not in any other person. Therefore, the essential meaning is that he who is the **Supreme Shiva** <term>Paramashiva; the ultimate, unmanifest aspect of God</term> is indeed the **Guru** <term>The spiritual preceptor, here identified as a divine principle</term>. This is also stated in the **Nirvana Tantra**:

> In the head-lotus resides the **Great God** <note>original: *Mahādeva*</note>, and likewise the **Supreme Guru** <note>original: *Paramo Guruḥ*</note>.
> There is none equal to him, O Goddess of the Gods, for he is to be worshiped in the three worlds.
> O Goddess, one should meditate on a portion of him as the four-fold Guru located externally.

This Supreme Shiva exists above the **Swan** <term>Hamsa; a symbol for the breath and the individual soul</term> which is described as being outside and below the triangle within the pericarp <note>the center of the lotus</note>. This is according to the words of the previously mentioned **Kankalamalini Tantra**:

> In that pericarp, O Goddess of the Gods, is the **Inner Soul** <term>Antaratma</term>, and then the Guru. There is the circle of the Sun and the circle of the Moon.

After describing the positioning in this sequence up to the **Great Shankhini Power** <note>original: *mahā-śaṅkhinī-śakti*</note>, it continues:

> Below that, O Goddess of the Gods, in the middle of the Moon's circle,
> Meditate there on a triangle; it is the digit <note>a "kala" or portion</note> whose nature is free from decay.
> In its center is the "Curved One" <note>the Kundalini</note>, the seventeenth digit, the friend of Liberation <note>original: *nirvāṇa-sakhyā-saptadaśī-kalā*</note>.

By these words, it is stated that the **Digit of Immortality** <note>original: *amā-kalā*</note> and the others reside within the triangle inside the Moon's circle. However, the Guru is said to be situated below that, above the **Inner Soul**. If one asks: "In the *Kankalamalini* text, the Guru's position is said to be above the Inner Soul; how then can it be said that the Guru is above the 'Swan'?"—the answer is that it is not a contradiction, because **Swan** and **Inner Soul** refer to the same thing. For instance, the meditation on the Guru in the *Kankalamalini Tantra* is as follows:

> In the center of the thousand-petaled lotus is the excellent **Inner Soul**.
> Above that, in the middle of the **Sound and the Point** <term>Nada and Bindu; the fundamental vibrations of creation</term>, is a brilliant throne.
> Upon that, one should eternally [meditate on] one's own Guru... and so on.

And elsewhere:

> In the inner altar made of mantra, one should remember one's own Guru in the form of Shiva,
> Preceded by his name and ending with the title "Lord of Bliss" <note>original: *amukānandanātha-antam*</note>.

Likewise, in the **Annadakalpa**:

> In the white, thousand-petaled lotus of the head, among the filaments, upon the bed of birds <note>original: *patatriṇāṃ talpe*; a poetic reference to the Hamsa/Swan</note>, is one's own Guru in the form of Supreme Shiva.

By a careful review of these various statements, the identity of the **Swan**...

It uses citations from the Nirvana and Kankalamalini Tantras to clarify the spatial hierarchy of the Sahasrara lotus, identifying the "Swan" (Hamsa) with the "Inner Soul" (Antaratma).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on the localization of the Supreme Guru and the Supreme Shiva within the Sahasrara (thousand-petaled lotus). It specifically addresses the spatial relationship between the triangle, the Bindu (point), and the Hamsa (Swan/soul), citing the Prapanchasara and Mahakali Tantra to define the nature of the divine duality of Purusha and Prakriti.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 64

The non-duality of the **Inner Soul** <term>Antaratman; the individual spark of divinity within</term> and the Supreme Soul is perceived. Thus, through the phrase "one’s own Guru who is the form of Supreme Shiva," one should realize that **Supreme Shiva** <term>Paramashiva; the ultimate, unmanifest aspect of God</term> himself is indeed the Guru. 

Regarding the passage beginning with the description of the thousand-petaled lotus: "In its center there is a triangle with the appearance of lightning. Within that are two points <term>Bindu</term> in the form of the eternal **Visarga** <note>original: *visarga-rūpam avyayam*; the visarga consists of two vertical dots in Sanskrit, here representing the creative "emission" or discharge of the universe into duality</note>. In the center of that, in the **Region of the Void** <term>Shunya-desha</term>, is Shiva, who is known as the Supreme." This statement serves to explain the location of the Supreme Shiva within the triangle. 

According to a different school of thought, the location of the Guru is within the triangle inside the pericarp <note>the central seed-vessel or "heart" of the lotus</note> of the upward-facing twelve-petaled lotus, which sits just below the pericarp of the thousand-petaled lotus. This is clarified in the **Hymn of the Fivefold Footstool** <note>original: *Pādukā-pañcaka-stotra*</note>. It does not refer to the Guru's presence in the triangle of the uppermost thousand-petaled pericarp. He remains in that form within another triangle below the middle triangle; otherwise, it would contradict the words of the **Kankalamalini Tantra** <note>a foundational text in this tradition of yoga</note>. 

The phrase "the **Lord of All** <term>Sarvesha</term> resides there" means that the Lord of everyone dwells within this pericarp. Now, if the residence of the Lord was already established by describing the residence of Supreme Shiva, why is the residence of the "Lord of All" mentioned again? To clarify this, the text qualifies the Lord of All with the term **Swan** <term>Hamsa</term>. This Lord of All is the "Hamsa," which follows the form of the sequential mantra. As stated in the **Essence of the Universe** <note>original: *Prapañcasāra*</note>:

> That which is known as "Reality" is pure consciousness. From its proximity to the light, desiring to create, it becomes dense and eventually attains the state of a **Point** <term>Bindu</term>. In time, that point divides in two. The right side is the Point, and the left side is the **Visarga**. Thus, the right and left parts are distinguished as male and female. The sound *Ham* is called the Point, and *Sa* is known as the Visarga. The Point is said to be the **Male Principle** <term>Purusha</term>, and the Visarga is remembered as the **Creative Power** <term>Prakriti</term>. The **Swan** <term>Hamsa</term> is composed of both the Male and the Creative Power, and this entire world is of that same nature.

This is stated very clearly in the first chapter of the **Mahakali Tantra**:

> Within the thousand-petaled lotus, in the Void adorned with divine gateways, in the middle of the **Circle of the Moon** <note>original: *candra-maṇḍala*</note>, above the two syllables of the **Swan** <term>Hamsa</term>, resembling pure crystal, pure...

<summary>This page clarifies the specific location of the Guru and Supreme Shiva within the anatomy of the thousand-petaled lotus, reconciling different Tantric texts. It explains that the "Swan" (Hamsa) represents the union of the male and female divine principles (Purusha and Prakriti), which form the basis of the manifest world.</summary>

<keywords>Supreme Shiva, Guru, Sahasrara, Triangle, Bindu, Visarga, Hamsa, Purusha, Prakriti, Prapanchasara, Mahakali Tantra, Twelve-petaled lotus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on the Sahasrara (thousand-petaled lotus), specifically focusing on the different names assigned to this supreme state by various sectarian traditions and the symbolic identity of the "Hamsa" (the Swan or Soul) situated there.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 62

Worshippers of Shiva <term>Shaivas</term> call this the **Place of Shiva** <note>original: *Śivasthānaṃ*</note>, while the assemblies of Vishnu worshippers <term>Vaishnavas</term> call it the **Supreme Person** <note>original: *Paramapurūṣaṃ*</note>. Others speak of it as the **Place of Hari-Hara** <note>original: *Hariharapadaṃ*; the combined form of Vishnu (Hari) and Shiva (Hara)</note>. The text continues: "The Supreme, shining like white silk," and so forth. Here, it is clearly expressed that this resides "above the two syllables of the *Hamsa*." 

Alternatively, it is well-known and recognized by the name **Supreme Swan** <term>Paramahamsa; a title for a liberated soul, literally 'Supreme Swan'</term>. For this reason, the author himself stated in the forty-eighth verse that "some call this by the name *Hamsa*." Or, considering the phrase "that which has *Hamsa* at its end" <note>original: *haṃso’nte yasya sa haṃso’ntaḥ*</note>, and that which is both the *Hamsa* and the Supreme, the name **Supreme Swan** <note>original: *Haṃsaḥ-parama*</note> is established through a grammatical rule where the middle term is dropped <note>original: *śākapārthivāditvāt madhyapadalope*; a specific Sanskrit linguistic convention for forming compounds</note>. By this, it means it is known by the title *Paramahamsa*.

This is precisely why it is said in the **Agama-kalpa-druma** <note>The 'Desire-Fulfilling Tree of the Scriptures,' a tantric manual</note>: "This is called the *Paramahamsa*, who pervades all things moving and unmoving." It is described as "the overflowing surge of the continuous waves of all types of bliss." **All types of bliss** <note>original: *sakala-sukha*</note> refers to happiness in every form; its **continuity** <note>original: *santāna*</note> is its expansion. That very expansion is the **surge of waves** <note>original: *laharī-parīvāha*</note> which is carried everywhere within the city <note>of the body</note> and is revealed as something that endures through subsequent time. The implication is that this *Hamsa* is characterized by every kind of bliss, is all-pervasive, and takes the form of a series of waves. As stated previously, this *Hamsa* resides below the **Supreme Shiva**. ॥ 43 ॥

The author now explains that because the *Hamsa*—who embodies all the deities—resides within the **pericarp** <note>original: *karṇikā*; the central seed-pod or heart of the lotus</note>, this is the place of the chosen deity for all worshippers, whether they follow Shiva, the Goddess <term>Shaktas</term>, or others. 

He says: **Place of Shiva** <note>original: *Śivasthānaṃ*</note>. This means that the followers of Shiva call this lotus the Place of Shiva. **Supreme Person** <note>original: *Paramapurūṣaṃ*</note> refers to the place of the Supreme Person, Vishnu <note>this is established here through a grammatical derivative</note>. Regarding the phrase **"some others"** <note>original: *kecidapare*</note>: those who worship the combined form of Hari-Hara, as well as those Shaivas and Vaishnavas who are distinct from the exclusive worshippers of Shiva or Vishnu, call it the **Place of Hari-Hara**. Thus, all Shaivas and Vaishnavas...

<summary>This page explains that the Sahasrara is the universal goal of all Hindu sects, though they call it by different names (Place of Shiva, Vishnu, or Hari-Hara). It also provides a grammatical and mystical analysis of the term "Hamsa" (Swan/Soul) as the all-pervading source of bliss.</summary>
<keywords>Sahasrara, Hamsa, Paramahamsa, Shiva, Vishnu, Hari-Hara, Agama-kalpa-druma, bliss, Shaivism, Vaishnavism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical commentary on the Sahasrara (the thousand-petaled lotus), moving from the various sectarian names of this state (Shiva, Vishnu, Hari-Hara) to the specific realization of the "Place of Nature and Spirit" and the spiritual fruits of this knowledge.</meta>

# Investigation into the Six Chakras 66

->...the great sages who taste the nectar of the lotus-like feet of the Goddess, and others as well, [call this] the stainless **Abode of Nature and Spirit**. — Verse 44<-

->Having known this place, the best of men, with his own mind firmly fixed, shall no longer exist in the cycle of rebirth, nor shall he be bound in the three worlds. [Verse 45]<-

By this, the author intends to show that they do not call it merely the **Abode of Shiva** <note>original: *Śivasthānaṃ*</note> or merely the **Abode of Vishnu** <note>original: *Viṣṇusthānaṃ*</note>. This is why it is said that "others" who are devoted to the worship of the Divine Couple <note>Shiva and Shakti combined</note> call it thus. 

Alternatively, other great sages who are worshippers of the **Hamsa Mantra** <note>original: *Haṃsa-mantra*; the 'breath' mantra of "I am He"</note> call it the **Abode of Nature and Spirit** <note>original: *Prakṛti-puruṣa-sthāna*</note>. This is because the *Hamsa* <term>literally 'Swan,' representing the soul or breath</term> is of the dual nature of both Nature and Spirit, and this is the place where it resides. This description is merely an illustration; it should be understood that since the Supreme **Point** <term>Bindu; the seed of all creation</term> resides there, and that Point contains all deities, a worshipper of any particular deity will call it the abode of that deity. ॥ 44 ॥

The text now describes the result of knowing the **Thousand-Petaled Lotus** <term>Sahasrara</term> with the phrase "this place." The inner meaning is that one gains a realization of this place in its entirety, including all its subtle parts and aspects. 

**With his own mind firmly fixed** <note>original: *niyata-nija-cittaḥ*</note> means that his mind is perpetually and steadily focused upon that state. **Shall no longer exist in the cycle of rebirth** means that he becomes liberated. The reason for this is given in the phrase **nor shall he be bound in the three worlds**. Here, "bound" means being tied by the snare of illusion <term>Maya</term> in the form of merit and demerit <note>original: *puṇya-pāpa*; the results of good and bad deeds</note>. 

This is stated in the **Bhagavata Purana** <note>A central scripture of the devotional tradition</note>: "While actions are being performed by the qualities of nature, the soul thinks itself the doer; this is its bondage to the cycle of rebirth and its state of dependence." 

Also in the **Bhagavad Gita** <note>The 'Song of the Lord'</note>: "O son of Kunti, one is bound by their own nature produced by their own past actions." 

Therefore, bondage consists of taking a physical body to experience the results of merit and demerit. This occurs in heaven to experience merit, in the underworld <note>Patala</note> to experience demerit, and in the mortal world for both. However, for those who know the ultimate truth, no further merit or demerit arises to cause future bondage, and their accumulated past merit and demerit are destroyed. At any place or any time...

<summary>This page explains that the Sahasrara is viewed by different traditions as the union of Nature (Prakriti) and Spirit (Purusha). It describes the spiritual reward of this knowledge as liberation from the cycle of rebirth and the "snare" of karma.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Verse 45 of the *Sat-cakra-nirupana*, detailing the supernatural powers and spiritual state of the practitioner who has mastered the Sahasrara.
